People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I’m a receptionist, so when I’m scheduling appointments or even working on the lobby displays, my mind kind of slips off track because I’m thinking about what I might’ve missed or overlooked. I just thought maybe I should talk to someone before it gets worse, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any difficulties with focus or feeling overwhelmed outside of your job as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly I’ve noticed it at work, honestly. Like when I’m scheduling appointments or trying to fix up the lobby displays, I’ll lose my place because I’m thinking, “Did I miss something? Did I overlook something?” It’s not constant or anything, but it’s enough that I thought maybe I should mention it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say it’s a lot, exactly, but yes, it does make me anxious. I catch myself worrying about ordinary things more than I probably need to—work schedules, bills, travel plans, even little paperwork mistakes. It’s like my mind jumps to, “What if this small mix-up turns into a bigger problem?” I know that might sound a little silly, but that’s kind of what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when they are going well?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Even when things are basically fine, I’ll still catch myself worrying about ordinary stuff—like the work schedule, bills, travel plans, or some tiny paperwork error. It’s like my mind goes to, “What if this one small mix-up turns into a much bigger problem?” I know that probably sounds like overthinking, but yes, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this kind of anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about nine months now. It started after that overnight detention thing, with the paperwork mix-up around it. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it was, but that’s when I noticed this kind of worrying really started to stick around.\n\nDoctor: Is this worrying happening in your personal relationships or just mainly at work?\n\nPatient: Mostly at work, I think… but not only work. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m worried all the time, but I do find myself worrying about ordinary things in general—bills, travel plans, little paperwork errors, schedules. It’s more like I get stuck on the idea that one small mix-up could turn into something bigger. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in particular that triggers your worrying or is it more of a general feeling that's always there?\n\nPatient: It’s… kind of both, I guess. There are certain things that set it off more—like work schedules, bills, travel plans, or if I notice some little paperwork error. But it’s not always one big obvious trigger. Not literally everything, but… I find myself worrying about all kinds of ordinary things now—work schedules, bills, travel plans, little paperwork errors. I’ll start thinking one small mix-up could turn into something much bigger. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control your worrying when it starts, or does it feel like it's getting away from you?\n\nPatient: I can usually talk to myself a little, like, “Sunny, it’s handled, you don’t need to keep thinking about it.” And sometimes that helps for a minute. But… honestly, it does kind of keep looping anyway. Then I’ll go from one concern to the next, and before I know it I’m checking details over and over in my head. Not in a dramatic way, I don’t think, but it does feel hard to shut off once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is disproportionate to the actual problems you're facing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but yes, probably. A lot of the time the thing itself is pretty ordinary—like a schedule change, a bill, travel details, or some tiny paperwork mistake—and my mind treats it like it could turn into a much bigger problem. I can usually see, logically, that it’s not as serious as I’m making it feel… but I still worry anyway.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying affected your ability to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like to do?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I’m still showing up and getting through the day, so I don’t want to overstate it. It’s more that things take me longer now. Like with scheduling at work, I double-check myself so much that it slows me down. And I’ve had a couple times where I had to redo parts of the lobby displays because I lost track of what I was arranging. It’s not terrible, I guess, but it does get in the way some.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. Sometimes it’s just ordinary things, and my mind jumps to, “What if this small mix-up turns into a much bigger problem?” I know that may sound a little excessive, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about a specific situation or topic, or is it more about anything and everything in general?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. Not literally everything, but… I find myself worrying about all kinds of ordinary things now—work schedules, bills, travel plans, little paperwork errors. I’ll start thinking one small mix-up could turn into something much bigger. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control your worrying when it starts, or does it feel like it's getting away from you?\n\nPatient: I can usually talk to myself a little, like, “Sunny, it’s handled, you don’t need to keep thinking about it.” And sometimes that helps for a minute. But… honestly, it does kind of keep looping anyway. Then I’ll go from one concern to the next, and before I know it I’m checking details over and over in my head. Not in a dramatic way, I don’t think, but it does feel hard to shut off once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is disproportionate to the actual problems you're facing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but yes, probably. A lot of the time the thing itself is pretty ordinary—like a schedule change, a bill, travel details, or some tiny paperwork mistake—and my mind treats it like it could turn into a much bigger problem. I can usually see, logically, that it’s not as serious as I’m making it feel… but I still worry anyway.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying affected your ability to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like to do?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I’m still showing up and getting through the day, so I don’t want to overstate it. It’s more that things take me longer now. Like with scheduling at work, I double-check myself so much that it slows me down. And I’ve had a couple times where I had to redo parts of the lobby displays because I lost track of what I was arranging. It’s not terrible, I guess, but it does get in the way some.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. I mean, I try not to put it on other people too much. My family might notice I ask a few too many “are you sure?” kinds of questions, but I don’t think it’s caused real conflict or anything. At least, I hope not. If that’s the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you interact with your friends or family since this worrying started?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. I might be a little more careful about what I say, or I might ask for reassurance more than I used to, but I haven’t noticed a big change in how I am with them. I still talk to them and see them normally, as far as I can tell. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like when I’m scheduling appointments or trying to fix up the lobby displays, I’ll lose my place because I’m thinking, “Did I miss something? Did I overlook something?” It’s not constant or anything, but it’s enough that I thought maybe I should mention it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say it’s a lot, exactly, but yes, it does make me anxious. I catch myself worrying about ordinary things more than I probably need to—work schedules, bills, travel plans, even little paperwork mistakes. It’s like my mind jumps to, “What if this small mix-up turns into a bigger problem?” I know that might sound a little silly, but that’s kind of what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when they are going well?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Even when things are basically fine, I’ll still catch myself worrying about ordinary stuff—like the work schedule, bills, travel plans, or some tiny paperwork error. It’s like my mind goes to, “What if this one small mix-up turns into a much bigger problem?” I know that probably sounds like overthinking, but yes, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this kind of anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about nine months now. It started after that overnight detention thing, with the paperwork mix-up around it. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it was, but that’s when I noticed this kind of worrying really started to stick around.\n\nDoctor: Is this worrying happening in your personal relationships or just mainly at work?\n\nPatient: Mostly at work, I think… but not only work. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m worried all the time, but I do find myself worrying about ordinary things in general—bills, travel plans, little paperwork errors, schedules. It’s more like I get stuck on the idea that one small mix-up could turn into something bigger. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in particular that triggers your worrying or is it more of a general feeling that's always there?\n\nPatient: It’s… kind of both, I guess. There are certain things that set it off more—like work schedules, bills, travel plans, or if I notice some little paperwork error. But it’s not always one big obvious trigger. Sometimes it’s just ordinary things, and my mind jumps to, “What if this small mix-up turns into a much bigger problem?” I know that may sound a little excessive, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about a specific situation or topic, or is it more about anything and everything in general?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. Not literally everything, but… I find myself worrying about all kinds of ordinary things now—work schedules, bills, travel plans, little paperwork errors. I’ll start thinking one small mix-up could turn into something much bigger. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control your worrying when it starts, or does it feel like it's getting away from you?\n\nPatient: I can usually talk to myself a little, like, “Sunny, it’s handled, you don’t need to keep thinking about it.” And sometimes that helps for a minute. But… honestly, it does kind of keep looping anyway. Then I’ll go from one concern to the next, and before I know it I’m checking details over and over in my head. Not in a dramatic way, I don’t think, but it does feel hard to shut off once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is disproportionate to the actual problems you're facing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but yes, probably. A lot of the time the thing itself is pretty ordinary—like a schedule change, a bill, travel details, or some tiny paperwork mistake—and my mind treats it like it could turn into a much bigger problem. I can usually see, logically, that it’s not as serious as I’m making it feel… but I still worry anyway.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying affected your ability to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like to do?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I’m still showing up and getting through the day, so I don’t want to overstate it. It’s more that things take me longer now. Like with scheduling at work, I double-check myself so much that it slows me down. And I’ve had a couple times where I had to redo parts of the lobby displays because I lost track of what I was arranging. It’s not terrible, I guess, but it does get in the way some.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. I mean, I try not to put it on other people too much. My family might notice I ask a few too many “are you sure?” kinds of questions, but I don’t think it’s caused real conflict or anything. At least, I hope not. If that’s the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you interact with your friends or family since this worrying started?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. I might be a little more careful about what I say, or I might ask for reassurance more than I used to, but I haven’t noticed a big change in how I am with them. I still talk to them and see them normally, as far as I can tell. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any activities or situations because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve been avoiding things, no… at least not anything obvious. I still go to work and do the usual errands and see people. I might hesitate a little more before doing something, but I wouldn’t say I’m really avoiding situations. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"intent": "determine presence of significant avoidance behavior", "question": "Have you found yourself avoiding certain tasks or responsibilities at work because of your worrying?"}

I might be a little more careful about what I say, or I might ask for reassurance more than I used to, but I haven’t noticed a big change in how I am with them. I still talk to them and see them normally, as far as I can tell. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.0, F43.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s probably not a huge thing, but… I’ve noticed at work I’m losing my place more than I’d like. I’m a receptionist, so when I’m scheduling appointments or even working on the lobby displays, my mind kind of slips off track because I’m thinking about what I might’ve missed or overlooked. I just thought maybe I should talk to someone before it gets worse, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any difficulties with focus or feeling overwhelmed outside of your job as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly I’ve noticed it at work, honestly. Like when I’m scheduling appointments or trying to fix up the lobby displays, I’ll lose my place because I’m thinking, “Did I miss something? Did I overlook something?” It’s not constant or anything, but it’s enough that I thought maybe I should mention it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say it’s a lot, exactly, but yes, it does make me anxious. I catch myself worrying about ordinary things more than I probably need to—work schedules, bills, travel plans, even little paperwork mistakes. It’s like my mind jumps to, “What if this small mix-up turns into a bigger problem?” I know that might sound a little silly, but that’s kind of what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when they are going well?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Even when things are basically fine, I’ll still catch myself worrying about ordinary stuff—like the work schedule, bills, travel plans, or some tiny paperwork error. It’s like my mind goes to, “What if this one small mix-up turns into a much bigger problem?” I know that probably sounds like overthinking, but yes, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this kind of anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about nine months now. It started after that overnight detention thing, with the paperwork mix-up around it. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it was, but that’s when I noticed this kind of worrying really started to stick around.\n\nDoctor: Is this worrying happening in your personal relationships or just mainly at work?\n\nPatient: Mostly at work, I think… but not only work. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m worried all the time, but I do find myself worrying about ordinary things in general—bills, travel plans, little paperwork errors, schedules. It’s more like I get stuck on the idea that one small mix-up could turn into something bigger. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in particular that triggers your worrying or is it more of a general feeling that's always there?\n\nPatient: It’s… kind of both, I guess. There are certain things that set it off more—like work schedules, bills, travel plans, or if I notice some little paperwork error. But it’s not always one big obvious trigger. Sometimes it’s just ordinary things, and my mind jumps to, “What if this small mix-up turns into a much bigger problem?” I know that may sound a little excessive, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about a specific situation or topic, or is it more about anything and everything in general?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. Not literally everything, but… I find myself worrying about all kinds of ordinary things now—work schedules, bills, travel plans, little paperwork errors. I’ll start thinking one small mix-up could turn into something much bigger. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control your worrying when it starts, or does it feel like it's getting away from you?\n\nPatient: I can usually talk to myself a little, like, “Sunny, it’s handled, you don’t need to keep thinking about it.” And sometimes that helps for a minute. But… honestly, it does kind of keep looping anyway. Then I’ll go from one concern to the next, and before I know it I’m checking details over and over in my head. Not in a dramatic way, I don’t think, but it does feel hard to shut off once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is disproportionate to the actual problems you're facing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but yes, probably. A lot of the time the thing itself is pretty ordinary—like a schedule change, a bill, travel details, or some tiny paperwork mistake—and my mind treats it like it could turn into a much bigger problem. I can usually see, logically, that it’s not as serious as I’m making it feel… but I still worry anyway.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying affected your ability to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like to do?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I’m still showing up and getting through the day, so I don’t want to overstate it. It’s more that things take me longer now. Like with scheduling at work, I double-check myself so much that it slows me down. And I’ve had a couple times where I had to redo parts of the lobby displays because I lost track of what I was arranging. It’s not terrible, I guess, but it does get in the way some.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. I mean, I try not to put it on other people too much. My family might notice I ask a few too many “are you sure?” kinds of questions, but I don’t think it’s caused real conflict or anything. At least, I hope not. If that’s the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you interact with your friends or family since this worrying started?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. I might be a little more careful about what I say, or I might ask for reassurance more than I used to, but I haven’t noticed a big change in how I am with them. I still talk to them and see them normally, as far as I can tell. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any activities or situations because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve been avoiding things, no… at least not anything obvious. I still go to work and do the usual errands and see people. I might hesitate a little more before doing something, but I wouldn’t say I’m really avoiding situations. I still do the things I’m supposed to do at work. I might take a little longer to double-check something before I move on, but I wouldn’t say I avoid tasks or responsibilities. At least, I hope it doesn’t come across that way there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind, even when you try to ignore them or make them go away?\n\nPatient: I mean… not like pictures or anything scary, really. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.0, F43.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s probably not a huge thing, but… I’ve noticed at work I’m losing my place more than I’d like. I’m a receptionist, so when I’m scheduling appointments or even working on the lobby displays, my mind kind of slips off track because I’m thinking about what I might’ve missed or overlooked. I just thought maybe I should talk to someone before it gets worse, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any difficulties with focus or feeling overwhelmed outside of your job as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly I’ve noticed it at work, honestly. Like when I’m scheduling appointments or trying to fix up the lobby displays, I’ll lose my place because I’m thinking, “Did I miss something? Did I overlook something?” It’s not constant or anything, but it’s enough that I thought maybe I should mention it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say it’s a lot, exactly, but yes, it does make me anxious. I catch myself worrying about ordinary things more than I probably need to—work schedules, bills, travel plans, even little paperwork mistakes. It’s like my mind jumps to, “What if this small mix-up turns into a bigger problem?” I know that might sound a little silly, but that’s kind of what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when they are going well?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Even when things are basically fine, I’ll still catch myself worrying about ordinary stuff—like the work schedule, bills, travel plans, or some tiny paperwork error. It’s like my mind goes to, “What if this one small mix-up turns into a much bigger problem?” I know that probably sounds like overthinking, but yes, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this kind of anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about nine months now. It started after that overnight detention thing, with the paperwork mix-up around it. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it was, but that’s when I noticed this kind of worrying really started to stick around.\n\nDoctor: Is this worrying happening in your personal relationships or just mainly at work?\n\nPatient: Mostly at work, I think… but not only work. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m worried all the time, but I do find myself worrying about ordinary things in general—bills, travel plans, little paperwork errors, schedules. It’s more like I get stuck on the idea that one small mix-up could turn into something bigger. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in particular that triggers your worrying or is it more of a general feeling that's always there?\n\nPatient: It’s… kind of both, I guess. There are certain things that set it off more—like work schedules, bills, travel plans, or if I notice some little paperwork error. But it’s not always one big obvious trigger. Sometimes it’s just ordinary things, and my mind jumps to, “What if this small mix-up turns into a much bigger problem?” I know that may sound a little excessive, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about a specific situation or topic, or is it more about anything and everything in general?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. Not literally everything, but… I find myself worrying about all kinds of ordinary things now—work schedules, bills, travel plans, little paperwork errors. I’ll start thinking one small mix-up could turn into something much bigger. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control your worrying when it starts, or does it feel like it's getting away from you?\n\nPatient: I can usually talk to myself a little, like, “Sunny, it’s handled, you don’t need to keep thinking about it.” And sometimes that helps for a minute. But… honestly, it does kind of keep looping anyway. Then I’ll go from one concern to the next, and before I know it I’m checking details over and over in my head. Not in a dramatic way, I don’t think, but it does feel hard to shut off once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is disproportionate to the actual problems you're facing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but yes, probably. A lot of the time the thing itself is pretty ordinary—like a schedule change, a bill, travel details, or some tiny paperwork mistake—and my mind treats it like it could turn into a much bigger problem. I can usually see, logically, that it’s not as serious as I’m making it feel… but I still worry anyway.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying affected your ability to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like to do?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I’m still showing up and getting through the day, so I don’t want to overstate it. It’s more that things take me longer now. Like with scheduling at work, I double-check myself so much that it slows me down. And I’ve had a couple times where I had to redo parts of the lobby displays because I lost track of what I was arranging. It’s not terrible, I guess, but it does get in the way some.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. I mean, I try not to put it on other people too much. My family might notice I ask a few too many “are you sure?” kinds of questions, but I don’t think it’s caused real conflict or anything. At least, I hope not. If that’s the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you interact with your friends or family since this worrying started?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. I might be a little more careful about what I say, or I might ask for reassurance more than I used to, but I haven’t noticed a big change in how I am with them. I still talk to them and see them normally, as far as I can tell. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any activities or situations because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve been avoiding things, no… at least not anything obvious. I still go to work and do the usual errands and see people. I might hesitate a little more before doing something, but I wouldn’t say I’m really avoiding situations. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain tasks or responsibilities at work because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I still do the things I’m supposed to do at work. I might take a little longer to double-check something before I move on, but I wouldn’t say I avoid tasks or responsibilities. At least, I hope it doesn’t come across that way there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind, even when you try to ignore them or make them go away?\n\nPatient: I mean… not like pictures or anything scary, really. It’s more thoughts that won’t let go. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” but then it just keeps looping, and pretty soon I’ve gone from one concern to another. Then I’m kind of checking the details in my head over and over, even when I know I probably don’t need to. Is that what you mean by recurring thoughts?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess… I’m still showing up and getting through the day, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But some things take me longer now. Like scheduling at work — I’ll double-check myself so much that it slows me down. And I’ve had to redo parts of the lobby displays because I kind of lost track of what I was arranging. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s probably not a huge thing, but… I’ve noticed at work I’m losing my place more than I’d like. I’m a receptionist, so when I’m scheduling appointments or even working on the lobby displays, my mind kind of slips off track because I’m thinking about what I might’ve missed or overlooked. I just thought maybe I should talk to someone before it gets worse, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any difficulties with focus or feeling overwhelmed outside of your job as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly I’ve noticed it at work, honestly. Like when I’m scheduling appointments or trying to fix up the lobby displays, I’ll lose my place because I’m thinking, “Did I miss something? Did I overlook something?” It’s not constant or anything, but it’s enough that I thought maybe I should mention it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say it’s a lot, exactly, but yes, it does make me anxious. I catch myself worrying about ordinary things more than I probably need to—work schedules, bills, travel plans, even little paperwork mistakes. It’s like my mind jumps to, “What if this small mix-up turns into a bigger problem?” I know that might sound a little silly, but that’s kind of what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when they are going well?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Even when things are basically fine, I’ll still catch myself worrying about ordinary stuff—like the work schedule, bills, travel plans, or some tiny paperwork error. It’s like my mind goes to, “What if this one small mix-up turns into a much bigger problem?” I know that probably sounds like overthinking, but yes, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this kind of anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about nine months now. It started after that overnight detention thing, with the paperwork mix-up around it. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it was, but that’s when I noticed this kind of worrying really started to stick around.\n\nDoctor: Is this worrying happening in your personal relationships or just mainly at work?\n\nPatient: Mostly at work, I think… but not only work. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m worried all the time, but I do find myself worrying about ordinary things in general—bills, travel plans, little paperwork errors, schedules. It’s more like I get stuck on the idea that one small mix-up could turn into something bigger. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in particular that triggers your worrying or is it more of a general feeling that's always there?\n\nPatient: It’s… kind of both, I guess. There are certain things that set it off more—like work schedules, bills, travel plans, or if I notice some little paperwork error. But it’s not always one big obvious trigger. Sometimes it’s just ordinary things, and my mind jumps to, “What if this small mix-up turns into a much bigger problem?” I know that may sound a little excessive, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about a specific situation or topic, or is it more about anything and everything in general?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. Not literally everything, but… I find myself worrying about all kinds of ordinary things now—work schedules, bills, travel plans, little paperwork errors. I’ll start thinking one small mix-up could turn into something much bigger. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control your worrying when it starts, or does it feel like it's getting away from you?\n\nPatient: I can usually talk to myself a little, like, “Sunny, it’s handled, you don’t need to keep thinking about it.” And sometimes that helps for a minute. But… honestly, it does kind of keep looping anyway. Then I’ll go from one concern to the next, and before I know it I’m checking details over and over in my head. Not in a dramatic way, I don’t think, but it does feel hard to shut off once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is disproportionate to the actual problems you're facing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but yes, probably. A lot of the time the thing itself is pretty ordinary—like a schedule change, a bill, travel details, or some tiny paperwork mistake—and my mind treats it like it could turn into a much bigger problem. I can usually see, logically, that it’s not as serious as I’m making it feel… but I still worry anyway.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying affected your ability to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like to do?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I’m still showing up and getting through the day, so I don’t want to overstate it. It’s more that things take me longer now. Like with scheduling at work, I double-check myself so much that it slows me down. And I’ve had a couple times where I had to redo parts of the lobby displays because I lost track of what I was arranging. It’s not terrible, I guess, but it does get in the way some.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. I mean, I try not to put it on other people too much. My family might notice I ask a few too many “are you sure?” kinds of questions, but I don’t think it’s caused real conflict or anything. At least, I hope not. If that’s the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you interact with your friends or family since this worrying started?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. I might be a little more careful about what I say, or I might ask for reassurance more than I used to, but I haven’t noticed a big change in how I am with them. I still talk to them and see them normally, as far as I can tell. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any activities or situations because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve been avoiding things, no… at least not anything obvious. I still go to work and do the usual errands and see people. I might hesitate a little more before doing something, but I wouldn’t say I’m really avoiding situations. At least, I hope it doesn’t come across that way there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind, even when you try to ignore them or make them go away?\n\nPatient: I mean… not like pictures or anything scary, really. And I’ve had to redo parts of the lobby displays because I kind of lost track of what I was arranging. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s probably not a huge thing, but… I’ve noticed at work I’m losing my place more than I’d like. I’m a receptionist, so when I’m scheduling appointments or even working on the lobby displays, my mind kind of slips off track because I’m thinking about what I might’ve missed or overlooked. I just thought maybe I should talk to someone before it gets worse, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any difficulties with focus or feeling overwhelmed outside of your job as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly I’ve noticed it at work, honestly. Like when I’m scheduling appointments or trying to fix up the lobby displays, I’ll lose my place because I’m thinking, “Did I miss something? Did I overlook something?” It’s not constant or anything, but it’s enough that I thought maybe I should mention it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say it’s a lot, exactly, but yes, it does make me anxious. I catch myself worrying about ordinary things more than I probably need to—work schedules, bills, travel plans, even little paperwork mistakes. It’s like my mind jumps to, “What if this small mix-up turns into a bigger problem?” I know that might sound a little silly, but that’s kind of what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when they are going well?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Even when things are basically fine, I’ll still catch myself worrying about ordinary stuff—like the work schedule, bills, travel plans, or some tiny paperwork error. It’s like my mind goes to, “What if this one small mix-up turns into a much bigger problem?” I know that probably sounds like overthinking, but yes, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this kind of anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about nine months now. It started after that overnight detention thing, with the paperwork mix-up around it. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it was, but that’s when I noticed this kind of worrying really started to stick around.\n\nDoctor: Is this worrying happening in your personal relationships or just mainly at work?\n\nPatient: Mostly at work, I think… but not only work. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m worried all the time, but I do find myself worrying about ordinary things in general—bills, travel plans, little paperwork errors, schedules. It’s more like I get stuck on the idea that one small mix-up could turn into something bigger. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in particular that triggers your worrying or is it more of a general feeling that's always there?\n\nPatient: It’s… kind of both, I guess. There are certain things that set it off more—like work schedules, bills, travel plans, or if I notice some little paperwork error. But it’s not always one big obvious trigger. Sometimes it’s just ordinary things, and my mind jumps to, “What if this small mix-up turns into a much bigger problem?” I know that may sound a little excessive, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about a specific situation or topic, or is it more about anything and everything in general?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. Not literally everything, but… I find myself worrying about all kinds of ordinary things now—work schedules, bills, travel plans, little paperwork errors. I’ll start thinking one small mix-up could turn into something much bigger. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control your worrying when it starts, or does it feel like it's getting away from you?\n\nPatient: I can usually talk to myself a little, like, “Sunny, it’s handled, you don’t need to keep thinking about it.” And sometimes that helps for a minute. But… honestly, it does kind of keep looping anyway. Then I’ll go from one concern to the next, and before I know it I’m checking details over and over in my head. Not in a dramatic way, I don’t think, but it does feel hard to shut off once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is disproportionate to the actual problems you're facing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but yes, probably. A lot of the time the thing itself is pretty ordinary—like a schedule change, a bill, travel details, or some tiny paperwork mistake—and my mind treats it like it could turn into a much bigger problem. I can usually see, logically, that it’s not as serious as I’m making it feel… but I still worry anyway.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying affected your ability to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like to do?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I’m still showing up and getting through the day, so I don’t want to overstate it. It’s more that things take me longer now. Like with scheduling at work, I double-check myself so much that it slows me down. And I’ve had a couple times where I had to redo parts of the lobby displays because I lost track of what I was arranging. It’s not terrible, I guess, but it does get in the way some.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. I mean, I try not to put it on other people too much. My family might notice I ask a few too many “are you sure?” kinds of questions, but I don’t think it’s caused real conflict or anything. At least, I hope not. If that’s the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you interact with your friends or family since this worrying started?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. I might be a little more careful about what I say, or I might ask for reassurance more than I used to, but I haven’t noticed a big change in how I am with them. I still talk to them and see them normally, as far as I can tell. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any activities or situations because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve been avoiding things, no… at least not anything obvious. I still go to work and do the usual errands and see people. I might hesitate a little more before doing something, but I wouldn’t say I’m really avoiding situations. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain tasks or responsibilities at work because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I still do the things I’m supposed to do at work. I might take a little longer to double-check something before I move on, but I wouldn’t say I avoid tasks or responsibilities. At least, I hope it doesn’t come across that way there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind, even when you try to ignore them or make them go away?\n\nPatient: I mean… not like pictures or anything scary, really. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s probably not a huge thing, but… I’ve noticed at work I’m losing my place more than I’d like. I’m a receptionist, so when I’m scheduling appointments or even working on the lobby displays, my mind kind of slips off track because I’m thinking about what I might’ve missed or overlooked. I just thought maybe I should talk to someone before it gets worse, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any difficulties with focus or feeling overwhelmed outside of your job as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly I’ve noticed it at work, honestly. Like when I’m scheduling appointments or trying to fix up the lobby displays, I’ll lose my place because I’m thinking, “Did I miss something? Did I overlook something?” It’s not constant or anything, but it’s enough that I thought maybe I should mention it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say it’s a lot, exactly, but yes, it does make me anxious. I catch myself worrying about ordinary things more than I probably need to—work schedules, bills, travel plans, even little paperwork mistakes. It’s like my mind jumps to, “What if this small mix-up turns into a bigger problem?” I know that might sound a little silly, but that’s kind of what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when they are going well?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Even when things are basically fine, I’ll still catch myself worrying about ordinary stuff—like the work schedule, bills, travel plans, or some tiny paperwork error. It’s like my mind goes to, “What if this one small mix-up turns into a much bigger problem?” I know that probably sounds like overthinking, but yes, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this kind of anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about nine months now. It started after that overnight detention thing, with the paperwork mix-up around it. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it was, but that’s when I noticed this kind of worrying really started to stick around.\n\nDoctor: Is this worrying happening in your personal relationships or just mainly at work?\n\nPatient: Mostly at work, I think… but not only work. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m worried all the time, but I do find myself worrying about ordinary things in general—bills, travel plans, little paperwork errors, schedules. It’s more like I get stuck on the idea that one small mix-up could turn into something bigger. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in particular that triggers your worrying or is it more of a general feeling that's always there?\n\nPatient: It’s… kind of both, I guess. There are certain things that set it off more—like work schedules, bills, travel plans, or if I notice some little paperwork error. But it’s not always one big obvious trigger. Sometimes it’s just ordinary things, and my mind jumps to, “What if this small mix-up turns into a much bigger problem?” I know that may sound a little excessive, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about a specific situation or topic, or is it more about anything and everything in general?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. Not literally everything, but… I find myself worrying about all kinds of ordinary things now—work schedules, bills, travel plans, little paperwork errors. I’ll start thinking one small mix-up could turn into something much bigger. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control your worrying when it starts, or does it feel like it's getting away from you?\n\nPatient: I can usually talk to myself a little, like, “Sunny, it’s handled, you don’t need to keep thinking about it.” And sometimes that helps for a minute. But… honestly, it does kind of keep looping anyway. Then I’ll go from one concern to the next, and before I know it I’m checking details over and over in my head. Not in a dramatic way, I don’t think, but it does feel hard to shut off once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is disproportionate to the actual problems you're facing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but yes, probably. A lot of the time the thing itself is pretty ordinary—like a schedule change, a bill, travel details, or some tiny paperwork mistake—and my mind treats it like it could turn into a much bigger problem. I can usually see, logically, that it’s not as serious as I’m making it feel… but I still worry anyway.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying affected your ability to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like to do?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I’m still showing up and getting through the day, so I don’t want to overstate it. It’s more that things take me longer now. Like with scheduling at work, I double-check myself so much that it slows me down. And I’ve had a couple times where I had to redo parts of the lobby displays because I lost track of what I was arranging. It’s not terrible, I guess, but it does get in the way some.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. I mean, I try not to put it on other people too much. My family might notice I ask a few too many “are you sure?” kinds of questions, but I don’t think it’s caused real conflict or anything. At least, I hope not. If that’s the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you interact with your friends or family since this worrying started?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. I might be a little more careful about what I say, or I might ask for reassurance more than I used to, but I haven’t noticed a big change in how I am with them. I still talk to them and see them normally, as far as I can tell. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any activities or situations because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve been avoiding things, no… at least not anything obvious. I still go to work and do the usual errands and see people. I might hesitate a little more before doing something, but I wouldn’t say I’m really avoiding situations. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain tasks or responsibilities at work because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I still do the things I’m supposed to do at work. I might take a little longer to double-check something before I move on, but I wouldn’t say I avoid tasks or responsibilities. At least, I hope it doesn’t come across that way there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind, even when you try to ignore them or make them go away?\n\nPatient: I mean… not like pictures or anything scary, really. It’s more thoughts that won’t let go. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” but then it just keeps looping, and pretty soon I’ve gone from one concern to another. Then I’m kind of checking the details in my head over and over, even when I know I probably don’t need to. Is that what you mean by recurring thoughts?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess… I’m still showing up and getting through the day, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But some things take me longer now. Like scheduling at work — I’ll double-check myself so much that it slows me down. And I’ve had to redo parts of the lobby displays because I kind of lost track of what I was arranging. So… yes, maybe it’s affecting things some, even if I’m managing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble sleeping due to your worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. I don’t want to overstate it, because I do sleep, but most nights I’ll get into bed tired and then my mind starts going over tomorrow’s appointments, or whether I missed some form or detail. And even when I do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I never really settled all the way. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying something that you experience every day?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… I mean, I don’t think it’s every minute or anything, but it does seem to come up almost every day. I know that probably sounds a little dramatic, but that’s sort of how it feels in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, a little. I’m still functioning, you know, I’m showing up and getting through the day, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart. But it does take some of the ease out of things. Scheduling at work takes me longer because I keep double-checking myself, and I’ve had to redo parts of the lobby displays when I lost track of what I was arranging. So I guess it does interfere some with my well-being… if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
